On global smooth solutions to the 2D isentropic and irrotational Chaplygin gases with short pulse data
Ding Bingbing, Xin Zhouping, Yin Huicheng 1. School of Mathematical SciencesMathematical Institute, Nanjing Normal University,Nanjing, 210023, China. 2. Institute of Mathematical Sciences, The Chinese University of Hong Kong, Shatin, NT, Hong Kong.

Abstract




Abstract

This paper establishes the global existence of smooth solutions to the 2D isentropic and irrotational Euler equations for Chaplygin gases with a general class of short pulse initial data, which, in particular, resolves in this special case, the Majda’s conjecture on the non-formation of shock waves of solutions from smooth initial data for multi-dimensional nonlinear symmetric systems which are totally linearly degenerate. Comparing to the 4D case,
the major difficulties in this paper are caused by
the slower time decay and the largeness of the solutions to the 2D quasilinear wave equation, some new auxiliary energies and multipliers are introduced to overcome these difficulties.

Keywords:
2D quasilinear wave equation, Chaplygin gases, short pulse initial data,
null condition,

inverse foliation density, Goursat problem

Mathematical Subject Classification:
35L05, 35L72

1
Introduction

1.1
The formulation of the problem and main results

Consider a general 2D quasilinear wave equation

\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi=0.

(1.1)

The general short pulse initial data for (
1.1
) are given as

\displaystyle\phi|_{t=1}=\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}\phi_{0}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\ \partial_{t}\phi|_{t=1}=\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\phi_{1}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),

(1.2)

where
\delta>0
is a small constant,
0<\varepsilon_{0}<1
is a fixed constant,
\omega=\frac{x}{r}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})(s,\omega)
are smooth functions defined in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
with compact support in the variable
s
. For small
\partial\phi
, set

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)=m^{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{\gamma=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi+\sum_{\gamma,\nu=0}^{2}h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\nu}\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi+O(|\partial\phi|^{3}),

(1.3)

where
m^{00}=-1
,
m^{ii}=1
for
1\leq i\leq 2
,
m^{\alpha\beta}=0
for
\alpha\neq\beta
,

g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}
and
h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\nu}
are constants. Then the first and second null conditions are defined as that for
\xi_{0}=-1
and
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma}\equiv 0,\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\nu=0}^{2}h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\nu}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma}\xi_{\nu}\equiv 0,\end{array}

(1.4)

respectively. It is emphasized that (
1.4
) is indispensable for the global existence of smooth solutions to (
1.1
)
even for the Cauchy problem with small initial data. Indeed, if (
1.1
) is equipped with the initial data

\phi(1,x)=\delta\varphi_{0}(x),\ \partial_{t}\phi(1,x)=\delta\varphi_{1}(x),

(1.5)

where
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})(x)\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then
it follows from
[
2
]
that
(
1.1
) has a global solution
\phi
when (
1.4
) holds.
Otherwise, when the first null condition or the second null condition is violated,
the smooth solution
\phi
can blow up in finite time as long as
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})\not\equiv 0
(see
[
1
]
,
[
3
]
,

[
6
]
,
[
13
]
and
[
29
]
).

We are interested in studying global solutions to (
1.1
) with the short pulse data (
1.2
) under suitable structural conditions, which turns out to be an important but difficult problem. To this end, we will focus in this paper on
the 2D compressible isentropic
irrotational Euler equations for Chaplygin gases
since the resulting second order quasilinear wave equation fulfills (
1.4
).
The 2D compressible isentropic Euler system for Chaplygin gases is

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho+div(\rho v)=0,\\
&\partial_{t}(\rho v)+div(\rho v\otimes v)+\nabla p=0,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(1.6)

where
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\partial_{2})=(\partial_{x^{1}},\partial_{x^{2}})
,
(t,x)\in[1,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
v=(v_{1},v_{2})
,
\rho
,
p

stand for the velocity, density, pressure
respectively, and the equation of state is given by

p(\rho)=P_{0}-\frac{B}{\rho}

(1.7)

with
P_{0}
and
B
being positive constants.
Supplement (
1.6
) with the irrotational initial data

\big(\rho,v)(1,x)=(\bar{\rho}+\rho_{0}(x),v_{0}(x)\big)=(\bar{\rho}+\rho_{0}(x),v_{1}^{0}(x),v_{2}^{0}(x)\big)

such that
\bar{\rho}+\rho_{0}(x)>0
and
\text{rot}\ v_{0}(x)=\partial_{2}v_{1}^{0}-\partial_{1}v_{2}^{0}\equiv 0
with
\bar{\rho}
being
a positive constant which can be normalized so that
c(\bar{\rho})=1
. Then the irrotationality,
\text{rot}\ v(t,x)=(\partial_{2}v_{1}-\partial_{1}v_{2})(t,x)\equiv 0
, holds true as long as the solution remains smooth. Hence there
exists a potential function
\phi
such that
v=\nabla\phi
, and the Bernoulli’s law,
\partial_{t}\phi+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+h(\rho)=0
,
holds with the enthalpy
h(\rho)
satisfying
h^{\prime}(\rho)=\frac{c^{2}(\rho)}{\rho}
and
h(\bar{\rho})=0
. In this case, for smooth
irrotational flows, (
1.6
) is equivalent to

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi=-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\triangle\phi+g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi+h^{\alpha\beta,\nu\gamma}\partial_{\nu}\phi\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi\\
\equiv&-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\triangle\phi-2\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\phi+2\partial_{t}\phi\triangle\phi-\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\partial_{ij}^{2}\phi+|\nabla\phi|^{2}\triangle\phi=0\end{split}

(1.8)

with
x^{0}=t
,
\partial_{0}=\partial_{t}
and
\Delta=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
, where
g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}
and
h^{\alpha\beta,\nu\gamma}
are corresponding constants. It is easy to check that (
1.8
)
satisfies both the null conditions in (
1.4
). Our main goal in this paper is to study the global smooth solution to (
1.8
) with the short pulse initial data (
1.2
).

Note that in order to guarantee the strict hyperbolicity of (
1.1
) or (
1.8
),
the smallness of
\partial\phi
should be required in general. On the other hand, as pointed out in
[
11
]
,
even for the 2D linear wave equation, the solution may have large first order derivatives if the initial first or
second order derivatives are large.
Therefore, for the
short pulse initial data (
1.2
), due to the largeness of
(\partial_{x}^{2}\phi,\partial_{xt}^{2}\phi)|_{t=1}

with small
\delta>0
, then it is difficult to keep the smallness of
\partial\phi
to (
1.1
) or (
1.8
) in general.
To maintain the smallness of
\partial\phi
, motivated by
[
24
]
and
[
11
]
, one can impose such an extra outgoing constraint
condition

(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}).

(1.9)

In addition, from the forms of (
1.2
) and (
1.9
), it naturally holds

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\Omega^{j}\partial^{q}\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}),\quad 0\leq k\leq 1,

(1.10)

where
\Omega=x^{1}\partial_{2}-x^{2}\partial_{1}
.
Here we point out that (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) are much weaker than the corresponding restrictions
in
[
11
]
for
0\leq k\leq 3
and in
[
24
]
for large
k
.
By suitable choices of
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
, (
1.9
) can be easily fulfilled
(see Remark
1.3
below).
It follows from (
1.2
) and (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) that
the short pulse data are some extensions of a class of large symmetric data, for
which the smallness restrictions are imposed on angular directions and along the “good” direction tangent
to outgoing light conic surface
\{t=r\}
, but the largeness is kept at least for the second order “bad” directional derivatives

\partial_{t}-\partial_{r}
. This provides a powerful framework to study the blowup or the global existence of
smooth solutions to multi-dimensional hyperbolic systems or second order quasilinear wave equations
with short pulse data, see
[
5
,
11
,
8
,
19
,
21
,
24
,
25
,
30
]
and the references therein.

Note that although the short pulse
initial data
(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)|_{t=1}
in (
1.2
) do not have the uniform boundedness (smallness)
in
H^{\frac{11}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

-norm (independent of
\delta
)
required for the well-posedness of regular solutions for 2D quasilinear wave equations
in
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
14
,
27
]
(see Remark
1.3
), yet the conditions (
1.2
) and (
1.9
)
imply the suitably stronger smallness of the directional
derivative
\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
of
\phi
. This is essential for our global existence
of smooth solutions to the Cauchy problem (
1.8
) with (
1.2
).

The main result of this paper can be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
hold and

(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})(s,\omega)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
be any fixed smooth functions with compact
supports in
(-1,0)
for the variable
s
. Under the assumption (
1.9
), there exists a
suitably small positive constant
\delta_{0}
such that for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
, the Cauchy problem,
(
1.8
) with (
1.2
), admits a global smooth solution

\phi\in C^{\infty}([1,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Furthermore, it holds that for all time
t\geq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

|\partial\phi(t,x)|\leq C\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-1/2},\ |\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{3/2-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{1/2},

(1.11)

where
C>0
is a constant independent of
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{0}
.

Some comments are in order.

Remark 1.1
.

Theorem
1.1
generalizes our previous global existence of smooth solution to general 4D quasilinear wave equation (satisfying the first null condition) with a class of short pulse initial data
[
11
]
to the 2D compressible isentropic irrotational Euler
equations for Chaplygin gases.

Remark 1.2
.

The results in Theorem
1.1
are motivated by the corresponding ones for the 2D homogeneous wave equation. Indeed, consider the Cauchy problem for the 2D wave equation
\Box\phi=0
with the short pulse initial data (
1.2
). By using the explicit solution formula, one can check easily that
\partial\phi
may be large in general due to the largeness of
\partial_{x}^{2}\phi|_{t=1}
and
\partial_{xt}^{2}\phi|_{t=1}
. However, as will be shown in Appendix, the smallness of
\partial\phi
with
|\partial\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-1/2}
for
t\geq 1
can be obtained provided that the condition (
1.9
) is satisfied.

Remark 1.3
.

The initial data (
1.2
) with (
1.9
) are essentially the “short pulse data” introduced by D. Christodoulou
in
[
5
]
, where it is shown that the formation of black holes in vacuum spacetime is due to the condensation of the
gravitational waves for the 3D Einstein equations in general relativity (see also
[
19
]
). Note that
properties (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) hold true for any given smooth function
\phi_{0}
and the choice of
\phi_{1}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega)=-\partial_{s}\phi_{0}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega)+\delta\psi(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega)

with any smooth fixed function
\psi(s,\omega)
and
supp_{s}\psi\subset(-1,0)
.
In addition, a large class of short pulse initial data with the property (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) with
0\leq k\leq 3

can be found for general second order quasilinear wave equations (see Section 2 of
[
11
]
).
It is noted that for short pulse data (
1.2
) and sufficiently small
\delta>0
, although both
||\phi||_{L^{\infty}}

and
||\partial\phi||_{L^{\infty}}
are small at
t=1
, yet the initial data are still regarded as “large” in the sense
that
|\partial^{2}\phi|
may be large, and meanwhile,

||\phi(1,\cdot)||_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=O(\delta^{\frac{5-\varepsilon_{0}}{2}-s})\rightarrow+\infty\ \text{as}\ \delta\rightarrow 0^{+}\ \text{for}\ s\geq\frac{11}{4}.

(1.12)

Remark 1.4
.

Theorem
1.1
implies in particular the uniform (independent of
\delta
) local in time well-posedness
for the Cauchy problem (
1.8
) with (
1.2
) and (
1.9
), which does not follow from the known results
[
14
,
18
,
27
]
.
Indeed, for the Cauchy problem of general 2D quasilinear wave equations with smooth coefficients,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}(w,\partial w)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}w=0,\\
&(w(1,x),\partial_{t}w(1,x))=(w_{0}(x),w_{1}(x))\in(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(1.13)

the local in time well-posedness of solution
w\in C([1,T],H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap C^{1}([1,T],H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

with
s>\frac{11}{4}
has been established in
[
27
]
. However, such a theory cannot be applied to (
1.8
)
with (
1.2
)
to yield the local well-posedness of smooth solution with time interval independent of
\delta
due to (
1.12
).

Remark 1.5
.

In
[
23
]
, A. Majda proposed the following
conjecture
: For any given
n
-dimensional nonlinear symmetric hyperbolic system with totally linearly
degenerate structures, a smooth solution, say in
C([0,T],H_{ul}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap C^{1}([0,T],H_{ul}^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
,
T>0
,

s>\frac{n}{2}+1
, will exist globally in time in general when the solution runs out of the domain of definition of the
Cauchy problem. In particular, the shock wave formation does not occur for any smooth initial data.

This conjecture is physically plausible and resolving it would provide insights into the nonlinear nature of the condition that requires linear degeneracy of each characteristic field. It would also help one understand how shock wave formation arises in quasilinear hyperbolic systems.

It is worth noting that our Theorem
1.1
solves Majda’s conjecture for (
1.6
) with irrotational short pulse initial data. However, the conjecture remains open for general small data of the form
(\rho,v)(1,x)=(\bar{\rho}+\varepsilon\rho_{0}(x),\varepsilon v_{0}(x))
with
\varepsilon>0
being small, unless certain symmetries are assumed. For more information, refer to
[
9
,
12
,
15
,
16
]
and the references therein.

Remark 1.6
.

Note that for the short pulse data (
1.2
), (
1.9
) ensures the smallness of
|\nabla\phi|
for small
\delta>0
so that (
1.8
) is hyperbolic. This is different from the case in
[
24
]
, where a global smooth solution was established for the 3D semi-linear wave system with certain short pulse data. In
[
24
]
, the short pulse data are required to satisfy
|(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\Omega^{q}\partial^{m}\phi|_{t=1}\lesssim\delta^{1/2-m}
(
\forall k\leq N_{0}
for large
N_{0}
) instead of (
1.10
).
Note that in general, as pointed out in
[
11
]
, the validity of (
1.10
) cannot be expected for large
k
since
\partial_{t,x}^{q}\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/2-|q|})
holds and (
1.10
) is over-determined for large
k\geq 3
. Additionally, it has been noted that due to the largeness of the integer
N_{0}
, the problem in
[
24
]
can be transformed into a small data one within the outermost outgoing cone. Recently, the authors in
[
30
]
proved the global existence of the relativistic membrane equation using a similar approach to
[
24
]
with short pulse initial data. It is worth mentioning that the relativistic membrane equation exhibits a special divergence structure and doesn’t contain the quadratic terms
g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\phi
whose time decay rate and the power of
\delta
are worse than that for the cubic terms
h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\nu}\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\phi
. However, in this paper, we have to overcome the difficulties of large values of
\phi
throughout the entire time-space
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the appearance of
g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi
, thus it seems difficult to modify the methods
in
[
24
]
and
[
30
]
to obtain the global solution for (
1.1
) or (
1.8
) with more general lower order short pulse initial data (
1.2
).

Remark 1.7
.

There have been extensive recent works on global well-posedness or finite time blowup of smooth solutions to various nonlinear multi-dimensional wave equations with short pulse initial data. In particular, the authors studied the 3D problem described by equation
-(1+3G^{\prime\prime}(0)(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2})\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\Delta\phi=0
in
[
25
]
. They found shock formation in finite time due to the genuinely nonlinear structure of the equation, which leads to the compression of incoming characteristic conic surfaces. This work serves as another main motivation for this work. Additionally, systematic results for general quasilinear wave equations of the form
-(1+(\partial_{t}\phi)^{p})\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\Delta\phi=0
(
p\in\mathbb{N}
) with short pulse initial data have been obtained in
[
8
,
21
]
, which show that there exists a critical power
p_{c}=\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon_{0}}

such that when
p>p_{c}
, the global solution
\phi
exists; when
1\leq p\leq p_{c}
,
the outgoing shock can be formed before the time
t=2
. On the other hand, by the similar analyses
in this paper together with
[
8
,
21
]
, for the 2D nonlinear
equation
-(1+(\partial_{t}\phi)^{p})\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\Delta\phi=0
(
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\geq 2
) with short pulse initial data,
one can derive that when
p>\tilde{p}_{c}=1+\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon_{0}}
, the global solution
\phi
exists, otherwise, the solution

\phi
blows up in finite time for
2\leq p\leq\tilde{p}_{c}
.

Remark 1.8
.

Note that for the short pulse data (
1.2
), the corresponding initial data
(\rho,v)
for (
1.6
) are small
perturbations of the non-vacuum uniform state
(\bar{\rho},0)
. For general large initial data, one cannot expect the
global existence of smooth solution to (
1.6
) in general. Indeed, even in 1D case, such a global existence of
smooth solution fails for large data as shown by the following example. Consider the following Cauchy problem of
the 1D compressible isentropic Euler equation for Chaplygin gases

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho+\partial_{x}(\rho v)=0,\\
&\partial_{t}(\rho v)+\partial_{x}(\rho v^{2}+p(\rho))=0,\\
&(\rho,v)(0,x)=\big(1,v_{0}(x)\big),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.14)

where
p(\rho)=2-\frac{1}{\rho}
,
v_{0}(x)\in C_{0}^{\infty}(-1,1)
and
v_{0}(x)\not\equiv 0
.
In terms of Lagrange coordinate
(s,m)
:

s=t,\quad m=\int_{\eta(t,0)}^{x}\rho(t,y)dy,

where
\eta(t,y)
is the particular path through
y
, (
1.14
) becomes

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{s}\rho+\rho^{2}\partial_{m}v=0,\\
&\partial_{s}v+\partial_{m}p(\rho)=0,\\
&\rho(0,m)=1,\quad v(0,m)=v_{0}(m).\end{aligned}\right.

Then the special volume
V=\frac{1}{\rho}
solves the following problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Box V=(\partial_{s}^{2}-\partial^{2}_{m})V=0,\\
&(V,\partial_{s}V)(0,m)=(1,v^{\prime}_{0}(m)),\end{aligned}\right.

whose unique solution is

V(s,m)=1+\frac{1}{2}\big(v_{0}(m+s)-v_{0}(m-s)\big).

(1.15)

It follows from (
1.15
) that one can choose
v_{0}
such that
v_{0}(m_{2})-v_{0}(m_{1})=-2
for
m_{1},m_{2}\in(-1,1)
with
m_{1}<0<m_{2}
. Hence for such initial data, there exists a
s^{*}\leq\frac{m_{2}-m_{1}}{2}
such that

\text{$0<V(s,m)$ for $s<s^{*}$},\min V(s^{*},m)=0,

which implies
\max\rho(s,m)\rightarrow\infty
as
s\rightarrow s_{-}^{*}
, so concentration occurs at
s^{*}
. Note that even for such
an example, the Majda’s conjecture in
[
23
]
still holds true since the singularity is the density concentration, not formation of shocks.
For more results on finite time blow up of smooth solutions to
n
-dimensional quasilinear wave equations with general large
data (not the short pulse data), we refer to
[
26
]
and the references therein.

Remark 1.9
.

We now make some brief comments on the analysis of the proof of Theorem
1.1
, the details are given in the next subsection. Although as for the 4D case in
[
11
]
, the main analysis consists of three parts: local estimates, global estimates near and inside the outermost outgoing cone respectively, yet the slower time decay in 2D makes the global estimates extremely difficult and much more involved for (
1.8
) besides the difficulties due to largeness of the solution compared with the case of 4D in
[
11
]
. It turns out that both the first and second null conditions are necessary for the global existence results here, which is contrast to 4D case where only the first null condition is required.
The key point in this paper is how to overcome the difficulties resulting from the slow time decay and largeness of the solution simultaneously, and thus new strategies applicable to (
1.8
) (including new auxiliary energies) are introduced in this paper.

Remark 1.10
.

For the general 2D quasilinear wave equation (
1.1
) satisfying the first and second null conditions and the short pulse initial data (
1.2
) satisfying (
1.9
)-(
1.10
), it is expected that the analogous results in Theorem
1.1
can be proved by combining the ideas developed here for (
1.8
) and the framework and estimates for the general 4D quasilinear wave equations in
[
11
]
. This will involve non-trivial and lengthy calculations and will be left for future.

1.2
Sketch for the proof of Theorem
1.1

Let
C_{0}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,t=r\}

be the outermost outgoing conic surface;
A_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,0\leq t-r\leq 2\delta\}
be a domain
containing
C_{0}
; and
B_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+\delta,t-r\geq 2\delta\}
be a conic domain inside
C_{0}
with the
lateral boundary given by
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,t-r=2\delta\}
.
At first, as in
[
11
]
, one can show the local existence of smooth solution
\phi
to
(
1.8
) with (
1.2
) and (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) for
t\in[1,1+2\delta]
and derive some basic properties for
the time
t=1+2\delta
on the domains
r\in[1-2\delta,1+2\delta]
and
r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta]
, respectively.
Then the main part of the analysis
consists of two parts: the global estimates of
the solution in
A_{2\delta}
, and the global estimates in
B_{2\delta}
.

1.2.1
Global estimates of the solution in
A_{2\delta}

As in
[
11
]
, motivated by the strategy of D. Christodoulou in
[
4
]
,
we start to construct the solution
\phi
of (
1.8
) in
A_{2\delta}
.
However, due to the slower time decay of solutions to the 2-D wave equation,
we need to introduce some
new auxiliary energies and carry out very elaborate analysis on the related nonlinear forms
by utilizing the distinguished characters of the resulting new
2-D quasilinear wave equations satisfying the first and second null conditions.

As in
[
4
]
or
[
28
]
, for a given smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.8
),
one can study the corresponding eikonal equation
\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha}{u}\partial_{\beta}{u}=0
with the initial data
u(1+2\delta,x)=1+2\delta-r
, and the inverse foliation density
\mu=-(\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}t)^{-1}
.
Under the suitable bootstrap assumptions on the smallness and time decay rate of
\partial\phi
(see
(\star)
in Section
3
)
and with the help of the null conditions for (
1.8
),

\mu
satisfies
\mathring{L}\mu=O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-3/2}\mu)
, where
\mathring{L}=-\mu\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}
is a vector field approximating
\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
.
Thus
\mu\sim 1
can be derived.
The positivity of
\mu
means that the outgoing characteristic conic surfaces never intersect as long as the smooth solution
\phi

exists. Set
\varphi=(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}):=\partial\phi=(\partial_{0}\phi,\partial_{1}\phi,\partial_{2}\phi)
. Then it follows from (
1.8
) that

\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=F_{\gamma}(\varphi,\partial\varphi),\quad\gamma=0,1,2,

(1.16)

where
g=g_{\alpha\beta}(\varphi)dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}
is the Lorentzian metric,

(g_{\alpha\beta}(\varphi))
is the inverse matrix of
(g^{\alpha\beta}(\varphi))
,
\Box_{g}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\det g|}}\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{2}\partial_{\alpha}(\sqrt{|\det g|}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta})
, and
F_{\gamma}
are smooth functions
in their arguments. To study the quasilinear wave system (
1.16
), we first focus on its linearization

\mu\Box_{g}\Psi=\Phi.

(1.17)

As in
[
11
]
,
it is crucial to derive the global time decay rate of
\Psi
where
\Psi=\Psi_{k}=Z^{k}\varphi
will be chosen in (
1.17
),
here
Z
stands for one of some first order vector fields. In this case, by computing
the commutator
[\mu\Box_{g},Z^{k}]
, there will appear the quantities containing the
(k+2)
-th order derivatives of
\varphi

in the expression
\Phi
of (
1.17
). For examples,
\not{\nabla}Z^{k-1}\chi
and
\not{\nabla}^{2}Z^{k-1}\mu
will occur in
\Phi
,
where
\chi=g(\mathscr{D}_{X}\mathring{L},X)
is the second fundamental form
with
\mathscr{D}
being the Levi-Civita connection of
g
and
X=\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta}

(
\vartheta
is the extended local coordinate of
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
which is described by

\mathring{L}\vartheta=0
and
\vartheta|_{t=1+2\delta}=\theta
). Following the analogous procedures
in Section 3-Section 11 of
[
11
]
and by a much involved analysis for the 2D problem (
1.8
)
with (
1.2
) and (
1.9
)-(
1.10
), we can eventually obtain
|\varphi|\lesssim\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-1/2}

and further close the basic bootstrap assumptions. Note that compared with the corresponding treatments for the
global 4D problem in
[
11
]
and the 3D
problem before the shock formation in
[
4
,
28
]
, it is more
difficult and complicated to derive the global
weighted energy estimates for the 2D system (
1.17
) with the suitable time decay rates due to the lower
space dimensions.
Indeed, to obtain the global weighted energy of
\Psi
, one can proceed as usual to
compute
\int_{D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}\mu\Box_{g}\Psi(V\Psi)
through appropriate choices of some first order vector field
V
,
where
D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}=\{(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):1\leq\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}<\mathfrak{t},0\leq u^{\prime}\leq u,0\leq\vartheta\leq 2\pi\}
.
We will choose the vector field
V
as, respectively,

\displaystyle J_{1}=-\varrho^{2m}\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\kappa,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\kappa}Q_{\kappa\beta}\mathring{L}^{\beta}\partial_{\alpha},\ J_{2}=-\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\kappa,\beta=0}^{2}g^{\alpha\kappa}Q_{\kappa\beta}\mathring{\underline{L}}^{\beta}\partial_{\alpha},

\displaystyle J_{3}=\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=0}^{2}\big(\frac{1}{2}\varrho^{2m-1}\Psi\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}\Psi-\frac{1}{4}\Psi^{2}\mathscr{D}^{\alpha}(\varrho^{2m-1})\big)\partial_{\alpha},

where
m\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4})
is a fixed constant,
\varrho=\mathfrak{t}-u
,
\mathring{\underline{L}}=-\mu(\mathring{L}+2\displaystyle\sum_{\nu=0}^{2}g^{\nu 0}\partial_{\nu})
, and
Q
is
the
energy-momentum tensor field
of
\Psi
given as

\begin{split}Q_{\alpha\beta}&=(\partial_{\alpha}\Psi)(\partial_{\beta}\Psi)-\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}\displaystyle\sum_{\nu,\chi=0}^{2}g^{\nu\chi}(\partial_{\nu}\Psi)(\partial_{\chi}\Psi).\end{split}

It should be pointed out that the weight
\varrho
acts as the time
{\mathfrak{t}}
in
J_{1}
and
J_{3}
because of

\rho\sim{\mathfrak{t}}
, and
the requirement of
m>\frac{1}{2}
in
J_{1}
is due to the slow time decay rate of solutions to the 2D wave equation
such that some related integrals are
convergent (see (
6.234
) in Section
6
, where
\int_{t_{0}}^{\mathfrak{t}}\tau^{-1/2-m}d\tau
will be
uniformly bounded for
{\mathfrak{t}}
). However,
once
m>\frac{1}{2}
is chosen, then
\int_{D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}\mu\Box_{g}\Psi(V\Psi)
with
V=J_{1}
and
J_{2}
will contain the non-negative integral
(m-\frac{1}{2})\int_{D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}\mu\varrho^{2m-1}|\not{d}\Psi|^{2}
which cannot be controlled by
the corresponding energies and fluxes on the left hand side of the resulting inequality. To overcome this crucial difficulty,
different from that in
[
11
]
, we will introduce
a new vector field
J_{3}
(which is inspired by
[
28
, (10.18c)]
). Thanks to the special structure of
J_{3}
and by some technical manipulations,
we can eventually obtain a new term
(m-1)\int_{D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}\mu\varrho^{2m-1}|\not{d}\Psi|^{2}

instead of
(m-\frac{1}{2})\int_{D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}\mu\varrho^{2m-1}|\not{d}\Psi|^{2}
on the right hand side of the related energy
inequality, which is non-positive for
m<1
and thus can be absorbed during the energy estimates. Meanwhile, in the estimate of

\int_{D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}\mu\Box_{g}\Psi(J_{3}\Psi)
, it is necessary to restrict
m<\frac{3}{4}
since the resulting integral
\int_{t_{0}}^{\mathfrak{t}}\tau^{2m-5/2}d\tau
is required to be
uniformly bounded (see (
4.143
)).

1.2.2
Global estimates of the solution in
B_{2\delta}

Inspired by the proof in
[
11
]
, the local existence result of equation (
1.8
) with (
1.2
) and (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) (see Theorem
2.1
) implies that for
r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta]
,

\partial^{\alpha}\phi
on the time
\{t=1+2\delta\}
admit the smallness of the higher order
\delta^{2-|\alpha|-\varepsilon_{0}}
.
In addition, note that the outgoing characteristic cones of
(
1.8
)
starting from
\{t=1+2\delta,1-2\delta\leq r\leq 1+\delta\}

are almost straight and contain
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
. By these properties, we can prove that on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
, the solution
\phi
and its derivatives satisfy
|\partial^{\alpha}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-1/2}
with the better smallness
O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}})
.
Based on such “good” smallness of
\phi
on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
, we will study the
global Goursat problem of (
1.8
) in the conic domain
B_{2\delta}
.
To this end, we intend to establish the global weighted spacetime energy estimates for the solution
\phi

in
B_{2\delta}
and make use of the modified Klainerman-Sobolev inequality to get the space-time decay rates for
\partial^{\alpha}\phi
,
which are different from that in
[
11
]
only for the time decay rate.
In addition, since the spacetime decay is slow in 2D, the energies in
[
11
]
are not enough, we then make use of the ghost weight
W=e^{2(1+t-r)^{-1/2}}
introduced in
[
2
]
to establish weighted spacetime energy estimates with the bootstrap assumptions. Based on this, we can obtain
the large space-time behaviors of
the solution
\phi
up to the forth
order derivatives (see Proposition
8.1
), for examples,
\partial\partial^{k}\phi=O(\delta^{3/2-\varepsilon_{0}-k})t^{-1/2}

and
\partial\partial^{k}\phi=O(\delta^{3/2-\varepsilon_{0}-k})t^{-3/2}(1+t-r)
with
k=0,1,2,3
(which imply that
\partial^{3}\phi
may be large in
B_{2\delta}
).

It is worth mentioning that the slow space-time decay of solutions to the 2D wave equation make it difficult to close the bootstrap assumptions directly since the corresponding higher order
energies of
\phi
may grow in time as
t^{2\iota}
(here
\iota>0
is some positive constant, see Theorem
8.1
).
To overcome this difficulty, we turn to studying the nonlinear equation for the error
\dot{\phi}=\phi-\phi_{a}
,
where
\phi_{a}
is the solution to the 2D free wave equation
\square\phi_{a}=0
with the initial data
(\phi(1,x),\partial_{t}\phi(1,x))
,
and obtain the uniformly controllable weighted spacetime energy estimates for
\phi
by a delicate analysis. In this case, the bootstrap energy
assumptions of
\phi
are closed, and then the global estimates of
\phi

with
|\partial\phi|\leq C\delta^{3/2-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-1/2}
inside
B_{2\delta}
are established.

1.3
Notations

Through the whole paper, unless stated otherwise, Greek indices
\{\alpha,\beta,\cdots\}
, corresponding to the
spacetime coordinates, are chosen in
\{0,1,2\}
; Latin indices
\{i,j,k,\cdots\}
, corresponding to the spatial
coordinates, are
\{1,2\}
; and the Einstein summation convention will be used.
In addition, the convention
f\lesssim g
means that there exists a generic positive constant
C
such that
f\leq Cg
.

Since (
1.8
) is a nonlinear wave equation,
it is natural to introduce the inverse spacetime metric
(g^{\alpha\beta})
as follows:

g^{00}=-1,\quad g^{i0}=g^{0i}=-\partial_{i}\phi,\quad g^{ij}=c\delta_{ij}-\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi,

(1.18)

while
(g_{\alpha\beta})
represents the corresponding metric:

g_{00}=-1+c^{-1}|\nabla\phi|^{2},\quad g_{i0}=g_{0i}=-c^{-1}\partial_{i}\phi,\quad g_{ij}=c^{-1}\delta_{ij},

(1.19)

where
c=1+2\partial_{t}\phi+|\nabla\phi|^{2}
, and
\delta_{ij}
is the usual Kronecker symbol.

Finally, the following notations will be used throughout the paper:

\displaystyle\omega^{i}:=\omega_{i}=\frac{x^{i}}{r},\quad i=1,2,\ \omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}),\ t_{0}=1+2\delta,

\displaystyle L=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r},\ \underline{L}=\partial_{t}-\partial_{r},\ \Omega=\epsilon_{i}^{j}x^{i}\partial_{j},

\displaystyle S=t\partial_{t}+r\partial_{r}=\frac{t-r}{2}\underline{L}+\frac{t+r}{2}L,

\displaystyle H_{i}=t\partial_{i}+x^{i}\partial_{t}=\omega^{i}\big(\frac{r-t}{2}\underline{L}+\frac{t+r}{2}L\big)+\frac{t\omega^{i}_{\perp}}{r}\Omega,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}=\{(t^{\prime},x):t^{\prime}=t,x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\},

where
\epsilon_{1}^{2}=1
,
\epsilon_{2}^{1}=-1
,
\epsilon_{i}^{i}=0
and
\omega_{\perp}=(-\omega^{2},\omega^{1})
.

2
Some preliminaries

2.1
Local existence of the smooth solution
\phi

In this subsection, we establish the local existence of the smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.8
)
with (
1.2
) and (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) for
1\leq t\leq t_{0}
. Meanwhile, some
key estimates of
\phi(1+2\delta,x)
on some special spatial domains are derived.

Theorem 2.1
.

Under the assumptions (
1.9
) and (
1.10
) on
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
, when
\delta>0
is suitably small, the
Cauchy problem, (
1.8
) with (
1.2
), admits a local smooth solution
\phi\in C^{\infty}([1,t_{0}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Moreover, for
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, it holds that

(i)

\displaystyle|L^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-2\delta,1+2\delta],

(2.20)

\displaystyle|\underline{L}^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta].

(2.21)

(ii)

|\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\quad r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta].

(2.22)

(iii)

|\underline{L}^{a}L^{b}\Omega^{k}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-2\delta,1+\delta].

(2.23)

Proof.

Although the proof is rather analogous to that of Theorem 3.1 in
[
11
]
, yet due to
the different structures between the general 4D quasilinear wave
equations satisfying the first null condition in
[
11
]
and the 2D quasilinear equation (
1.8
) fulfilling
both the first and second null conditions, we still give the details
for the reader’s convenience.

Denote by
Z_{g}
any fixed vector field in
\{S,H_{i},i=1,2\}
. Suppose that for
1\leq t\leq t_{0}

and
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
N_{0}\geq 6
,

|\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}Z_{g}^{a_{0}}\phi|\leq\delta^{3/2-|q|}\quad(|q|+k+a_{0}\leq N_{0},\quad a_{0}\leq 1).

(2.24)

Define the following energy for (
1.8
) and for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

M_{n}(t)=\sum_{|q|+k+a_{0}\leq n}\delta^{2|q|}\|\partial\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}Z_{g}^{a_{0}}\phi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Let
w=\delta^{|q|}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}Z_{g}^{a_{0}}\phi\quad(|q|+k+a_{0}\leq 2N_{0}-2,a_{0}\leq 1)
. It follows from (
1.18
), (
1.8
)
and integration by parts that

\begin{split}&\int_{1}^{t}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\partial_{t}wg^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}w)(\tau,x)dxd\tau\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\big(-(\partial_{t}w)^{2}-g^{ij}\partial_{i}w\partial_{j}w\big)(t,x)dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma_{1}}\big(-(\partial_{t}w)^{2}-g^{ij}\partial_{i}w\partial_{j}w\big)(1,x)dx\\
&+\int_{1}^{t}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big(-\partial_{i}g^{0i}(\partial_{t}w)^{2}-(\partial_{i}g^{ij})\partial_{j}w\partial_{t}w+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}g^{ij})\partial_{i}w\partial_{j}w\big)(\tau,x)dxd\tau\end{split}

(2.25)

with

\begin{split}g^{\alpha\beta}&\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}w=\delta^{|q|}\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}|q_{1}|+|q_{2}|\leq|q|,\ k_{1}+k_{2}\leq k,\ a_{1}+a_{2}\leq a_{0},\\
|q_{2}|+k_{2}+a_{2}<|q|+k+a_{0}\end{array}}(\partial\partial^{q_{1}}\Omega^{k_{1}}Z_{g}^{a_{1}}\phi)(\partial^{2}\partial^{q_{2}}\Omega^{k_{2}}Z_{g}^{a_{2}}\phi)\\
&+\delta^{|q|}\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}|q_{1}|+|q_{2}|+|q_{3}|\leq|q|,\\
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}\leq k,\\
\ a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}\leq a_{0},\\
|q_{3}|+k_{3}+a_{3}<|q|+k+a_{0}\end{array}}(\partial\partial^{q_{1}}\Omega^{k_{1}}Z_{g}^{a_{1}}\phi)(\partial\partial^{q_{2}}\Omega^{k_{2}}Z_{g}^{a_{2}}\phi)(\partial^{2}\partial^{q_{3}}\Omega^{k_{3}}Z_{g}^{a_{3}}\phi),\end{split}

(2.26)

where the unnecessary constant coefficients in (
2.26
) have been neglected.

It follows from the assumption (
2.24
) and (
2.25
) that

\begin{split}&\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\big((\partial_{t}w)^{2}+|\nabla w|^{2}\big)(t,x)dx\\
\lesssim&\int_{\Sigma_{1}}\big((\partial_{t}w)^{2}+|\nabla w|^{2}\big)(1,x)dx+\int_{1}^{t}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\delta^{-1/2}\big((\partial_{t}w)^{2}+|\nabla w|^{2}\big)(\tau,x)dxd\tau\\
&+\int_{1}^{t}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}|(\partial_{t}wg^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}w)|(\tau,x)dxd\tau.\end{split}

(2.27)

Using the bootstrap assumption (
2.24
) to estimate (
2.26
) and substituting
the resulting estimates into (
2.27
), one can get from the Gronwall’s inequality that for
1\leq t\leq t_{0}
,

M_{2N_{0}-3}(t)\lesssim M_{2N_{0}-3}(1)e^{\delta^{-1/2}(t-1)}\lesssim\delta^{3-2\varepsilon_{0}}.

Next, it follows from the following Sobolev’s imbedding theorem on the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{r}

(with center at the origin and radius
r
):

|w(t,x)|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\|\Omega^{\leq 1}w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{r})},

together with
r\sim 1
for
t\in[1,t_{0}]
and
(t,x)\in\textrm{supp}\ w
that

|\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}Z_{g}^{a_{0}}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\|\Omega^{\leq 1}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}Z_{g}^{a_{0}}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{r})}\lesssim\delta^{1/2}\|\partial\Omega^{\leq 1}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}Z_{g}^{a_{0}}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad

(2.28)

when
|q|+k+a_{0}\leq N_{0}
and
a_{0}\leq 1
, where
N_{0}\geq 6
has been used. Therefore, (
2.24
) can be closed
for suitably small
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
.

This, together with
\displaystyle L=(t+r)^{-1}(S+\omega^{i}H_{i})
, yields

|L^{a_{0}}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim|Z_{g}^{a_{0}}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}

(2.29)

with
|q|+k+a_{0}\leq N_{0}
and
a_{0}\leq 1
.

Figure 1:
Space-time domain
D^{1}=\{(t,r):1\leq t\leq t_{0},2-t\leq r\leq t\}

Now we improve the
L^{\infty}
estimate of
\phi(t_{0},x)
on some special domains. Rewrite (
1.8
) as

L\underline{L}\phi=\frac{1}{2r}L\phi-\frac{1}{2r}\underline{L}\phi+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Omega^{2}\phi+g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi+h^{\alpha\beta,\nu\gamma}\partial_{\nu}\phi\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi.

(2.30)

Acting the operator
L
on both sides of (
2.30
) yields an expression of
\underline{L}L^{2}\phi
by
\underline{L}L=L\underline{L}
and direct computations.
It can be checked easily that the worst term in the expression of
\underline{L}L^{2}\phi

is
L(g^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi)
. Then one can use (
2.29
) to get
|\underline{L}L^{2}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{1-2\varepsilon_{0}}
since (
1.8
) satisfies both null conditions. Using this together with
the vanishing property of
\phi
on
C_{0}
,
one can integrate
\underline{L}L^{2}\phi
along integral curves of
\underline{L}
to show that for
(t,x)\in D^{1}

(see Figure
1
),

|L^{2}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-2\varepsilon_{0}}.

(2.31)

Similarly, it holds that for
(t,x)\in D^{1}
,

|L^{2}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-2\varepsilon_{0}}\quad\text{for $|q|+k\leq N_{0}-4$}.

(2.32)

Substituting (
2.29
) and (
2.32
) into the expression of
\underline{L}L^{2}\phi
again, and noting that the worst term
in the expression of
\underline{L}L^{2}\phi
becomes
-\frac{1}{2r}L\underline{L}\phi
, one then further gets
|\underline{L}L^{2}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}

for
(t,x)\in D^{1}
by (
2.29
). Hence the following improved smallness estimate holds:

|L^{2}\phi|_{D^{1}}\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}\quad\text{for $1-2\delta\leq r\leq 1+2\delta$}.

Similar arguments show that

|L^{2}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi|_{D^{1}}\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad\text{for}\ |q|+k\leq N_{0}-5\ \text{and}\ 1-2\delta\leq r\leq 1+2\delta.

Analogously, an induction argument yields that for
r\in[1-2\delta,1+2\delta]
,

|LL^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi|_{D^{1}}\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad 3a+|q|+k\leq N_{0}-2.

(2.33)

Figure 2:
Space domain for
1-3\delta\leq r\leq 1+\delta
on
\Sigma_{1+2\delta}

Similarly, by the expression of
L\underline{L}^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi
, integrating
along integral curves of
L
yields that for
r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta]
(see Figure
2
below),

|\underline{L}^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi|_{D^{2}}\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad 2a+|q|+k\leq N_{0}-1.

(2.34)

On the other hand, it follows from (
2.29
) that

|\Omega^{k}\phi|_{D^{2}}\lesssim\delta^{3-\varepsilon_{0}},\ k\leq N_{0}-1

(2.35)

after integrating
L\Omega^{k}\phi
along integral curves of
L
in
D^{2}
.

Therefore, it follows from

\partial_{t}=\frac{1}{2}(L+\underline{L})
,
\partial_{i}=\frac{\omega^{i}}{2}(L-\underline{L})+\frac{\omega^{i}_{\perp}}{r}\Omega
,
(
2.29
) and (
2.34
)-(
2.35
) that for
|q|+k\leq N_{0}-3

and
r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta]
,

|\partial^{q}\Omega^{k}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}.

(2.36)

Finally, we show (
2.23
). Note that (
2.36
) implies that on the
surface
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
with
r\in[1-2\delta,1+\delta]
,
|\underline{L}^{\leq 1}\Omega^{k}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}

and
|L^{\leq 1}\Omega^{k}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}
for
k\leq N_{0}-5
, and
hence
|L\underline{L}\Omega^{k}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}
by (
2.30
).
Furthermore, we claim that

|\underline{L}^{a}L^{b}\Omega^{k}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad\text{for}\ 3a+3b+k\leq N_{0}-1,

(2.37)

which can be proved by induction. Indeed, assume that (
2.37
) holds for
a+b\leq n_{0}

with
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
3n_{0}+k\leq N_{0}-1
. One needs to verify the estimate
in (
2.37
) for
3(n_{0}+1)+k\leq N_{0}
and
a+b=n_{0}
. If
a\geq 1
, by (
2.30
) and the induction assumption, one can get that

\begin{split}&|\underline{L}^{a}L^{b+1}\Omega^{k}\phi|=|\underline{L}^{a-1}L^{b}\Omega^{k}(L\underline{L}\phi)|\\
\lesssim&\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}+\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}|\underline{L}^{a+1}L^{b}\Omega^{\leq k}\phi|+\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}|\underline{L}^{a-1}L^{b+2}\Omega^{\leq k}\phi|.\end{split}

This together with an induction argument yields

|\underline{L}^{a}L^{b+1}\Omega^{k}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}+\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}|\underline{L}^{a+1}L^{b}\Omega^{\leq k}\phi|.

(2.38)

If
b\geq 1
, similar to the proof of (
2.38
), one has

|\underline{L}^{a+1}L^{b}\Omega^{k}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}+\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}|\underline{L}^{a}L^{b+1}\Omega^{\leq k}\phi|.

(2.39)

Combining (
2.38
) with (
2.39
) yields

|\underline{L}^{a}L^{b+1}\Omega^{k}\phi|+|\underline{L}^{a+1}L^{b}\Omega^{k}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}},

which implies (
2.23
). Therefore, the proof of Theorem
2.1
is finished.
∎

2.2
The related geometry and definitions

In this subsection, we list some related geometric facts and definitions, which will be
utilized as basic tools later on. It is assumed that a smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.8
) is given.
The
“optical function”
corresponding to (
1.8
) can be defined as in
[
4
]

(see also Definition 3.4 of
[
28
]
).

Definition 2.1
.

For a given smooth solution
\phi
to the equation (
1.8
),
a
C^{1}
function
{u}(t,x)
is called an optical function
if
{u}
satisfies the eikonal equation

g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}{u}\partial_{\beta}{u}=0.

(2.40)

Choose the initial data
{u}(t_{0},x)=1+2\delta-r
and impose the condition
\partial_{t}u>0

for (
2.40
).
For a given optical function, the
inverse foliation density
\mu
of the
outgoing cones is defined as

\mu:=-\frac{1}{g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}t}\quad(=-\frac{1}{g^{\alpha 0}\partial_{\alpha}u}).

(2.41)

\mu\geq C>0
will be shown as long as the smooth solution

\phi
to (
1.8
) exists. We adopt most of terminologies and definitions in
[
4
]

(see also
[
28
]
).

Note that

\tilde{L}=-g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}

is a tangent vector field for the outgoing light cone
\{u=C\}
. In addition,
\tilde{L}t=\mu^{-1}
.
Then it is natural to rescale
\tilde{L}
as

\mathring{L}=\mu\tilde{L},

which approximates
L=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
. To obtain an approximate vector field of the incoming light cone,
one sets
\tilde{T}=-g^{\nu 0}\partial_{\nu}-\mathring{L}
, which is near
-\partial_{r}
for
t=t_{0}
. Then in order
to define a null frame, one can set

T=\mu\tilde{T},\quad\mathring{\underline{L}}=\mu\mathring{L}+2T,

where
\mathring{L}
and
\mathring{\underline{L}}
are two vector fields in the null frame.
Finally, the third vector field

X
in the null frame can be constructed by using
\mathring{L}
. Extending the local coordinate
\theta
on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
as

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathring{L}\vartheta=0,\\
&\vartheta|_{t=t_{0}}=\theta.\end{aligned}\right.

Subsequently, let
X=\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta}
. Then
X
is a tangent vector on
S_{\mathfrak{t},u}
. Rewrite
X=X^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
.
Then
X^{0}=0
holds due to
\frac{\partial t}{\partial\vartheta}=0
.

Lemma 2.1
.

\{\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}},X\}
constitutes a null frame with respect to the metric
(g_{\alpha\beta})
, and admits the following identities:

\displaystyle g(\mathring{L},\mathring{L})=g(\mathring{\underline{L}},\mathring{\underline{L}})=g(\mathring{L},X)=g(\mathring{\underline{L}},X)=0,

(2.42)

\displaystyle g(\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}})=-2\mu.

(2.43)

In addition,

\displaystyle\mathring{L}t=1,\quad\mathring{L}u=0,

(2.44)

\displaystyle\mathring{\underline{L}}t=\mu,\quad\mathring{\underline{L}}u=2.

(2.45)

And

\displaystyle g(\mathring{L},T)=-\mu,\quad g(T,T)=\mu^{2},

(2.46)

\displaystyle Tt=0,\quad Tu=1.

(2.47)

As in
[
28
,
25
]
, one can perform the change of coordinates:

(t,x^{1},x^{2})\longrightarrow(\mathfrak{t},u,\vartheta)
near
C_{0}
with

(\mathfrak{t},u,\vartheta)=(t,u(t,x),\vartheta(t,x)).

(2.48)

In the new coordinate
(\mathfrak{t},u,\vartheta)
, the following subsets are introduced (see Figure
3
below):

Definition 2.2
.

Set

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}^{u}:=\{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{t},0\leq u^{\prime}\leq u\},\quad u\in[0,4\delta],

\displaystyle C_{u}:=\{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\geq t_{0},u^{\prime}=u\},

\displaystyle C_{u}^{\mathfrak{t}}:=\{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):t_{0}\leq{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\leq{\mathfrak{t}},u^{\prime}=u\},

\displaystyle S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}:=\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}\cap C_{u},

\displaystyle D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}:=\{({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):t_{0}\leq{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}<{\mathfrak{t}},0\leq u^{\prime}\leq u\}.

Figure 3:
The indications of some domains

Next, the following geometric notations which will be used frequently.

Definition 2.3
.

For the metric
g
on the spacetime,

•

\underline{g}=(g_{ij})
is defined as the induced metric of
g
on
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}
, i.e.,

\underline{g}(U,V)=g(U,V)
for any tangent vectors
U
and
V
of
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}
;

•

\not{\Pi}_{\alpha}^{\beta}:=\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}-\delta_{\alpha}^{0}\mathring{L}^{\beta}+\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\tilde{T}^{\beta}
is the
projection tensor field on
S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
of type
(1,1)
, where
\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
is the Kronecker delta;

•

For any
(m,n)
-type
spacetime tensor field

\xi
,
\not{\xi}=\not{\Pi}\xi
is the tensor field on
S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
with components

\not{\xi}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}:=(\not{\Pi}\xi)^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}=\not{\Pi}_{\beta_{1}}^{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots\not{\Pi}_{\beta_{n}}^{\beta_{n}^{\prime}}\not{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\not{\Pi}_{\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{m}}\xi^{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\beta_{n}^{\prime}}.

In particular,
\not{g}=(\not{g}_{\alpha\beta})
is the induced metric of
g
on
S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
;

•

(\not{g}^{XX})
is defined as the inverse of
\not{g}_{XX}
with
\not{g}_{XX}=g(X,X)
;

•

\mathscr{D}
and
\not{\nabla}
denote the Levi-Civita connections of
g
and
\not{g}
, respectively;

•

\Box_{g}:=g^{\alpha\beta}\mathscr{D}^{2}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\not{\triangle}:=\not{g}^{XX}\not{\nabla}^{2}_{X}
;

•

\mathcal{L}_{V}\xi
is the Lie derivative of
\xi
with respect to
V

and
\not{\mathcal{L}}_{V}\xi:=\not{\Pi}(\mathcal{L}_{V}\xi)
for any tensor field
\xi
and vector
V
;

•

For any
(m,n)
-type spacetime tensor field
\xi
,

|\xi|^{2}:=g_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots g_{\alpha_{m}\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}g^{\beta_{1}\beta_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots g^{\beta_{n}\beta_{n}^{\prime}}\xi_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}\xi_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\beta_{n}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\alpha_{m}^{\prime}};

•

\text{div}U:=\mathscr{D}_{\alpha}U^{\alpha}
for any vectorfield
U
;
\not{\text{div}}Y:=\not{\nabla}_{X}Y^{X}
and
\not{\text{div}}\kappa:=\not{\nabla}^{X}\kappa_{X}
are angular divergence for any vectorfield
Y
and one form
\kappa
on
S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
.

Under the frame
\{\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}},X\}
,
the second fundamental forms

\chi

and
\tilde{\theta}
can be defined as

\chi_{XX}=g(\mathscr{D}_{X}\mathring{L},X),\quad\tilde{\theta}_{XX}=g(\mathscr{D}_{X}\tilde{T},X).

(2.49)

Define
one-form tensors

\zeta
and
\xi
as

\zeta_{X}=g(\mathscr{D}_{X}\mathring{L},\tilde{T}),\quad\xi_{X}=-g(\mathscr{D}_{X}T,\mathring{L}).

(2.50)

Then
\mu\zeta_{X}=-X\mu+\xi_{X}
.
For any vector field
V
, denote its associate
deformation tensor
by

{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(V)}\pi_{\alpha\beta}=g(\mathscr{D}_{\alpha}V,\partial_{\beta})+g(\mathscr{D}_{\beta}V,\partial_{\alpha}).

(2.51)

On the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{t_{0}}^{4\delta}
, one has that
\tilde{T}^{i}=\displaystyle-\frac{x^{i}}{r}+O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}})
,

\mathring{L}^{0}=1
,
\mathring{L}^{i}=\displaystyle\frac{x^{i}}{r}+O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}})
and

\chi_{XX}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\not{g}_{XX}+O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}})
.
Note that on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
,
r
is just
t_{0}-u
. For
\mathfrak{t}\geq t_{0}
, we
define the “
error vectors
” with the components being

\begin{split}&\check{L}^{0}:=0,\ \check{L}^{i}:=\mathring{L}^{i}-\frac{x^{i}}{\varrho},\ \check{T}^{i}:=\tilde{T}^{i}+\frac{x^{i}}{\varrho},\\
&\check{\chi}_{XX}:=\chi_{XX}-\frac{1}{\varrho}\not{g}_{XX},\end{split}

(2.52)

where
\varrho:={\mathfrak{t}}-u
.

Note that
\vartheta
is the coordinate on
S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
.
Then in the new coordinate system
({\mathfrak{t}},u,\vartheta)
,
one has
\mathring{L}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathfrak{t}}
. In addition, it follows from (
2.47
) that
T=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}-\eta^{X}X

for some smooth function
\eta^{X}
. And moreover, a similar analysis as for Lemma 3.66 of
[
28
]
gives that

Lemma 2.2
.

In domain
D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
, the Jacobian determinant of map
({\mathfrak{t}},u,\vartheta)\rightarrow(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2})
is

\det\frac{\partial(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2})}{\partial({\mathfrak{t}},u,\vartheta)}=\mu(\det\underline{g})^{-1/2}\sqrt{\not{g}_{XX}}.

(2.53)

Remark 2.1
.

(
2.53
) implies that for regular metrics
\underline{g}
and
\not{g}
, i.e.,
\det\underline{g}>0

and
\not{g}_{XX}>0
, the transformation of coordinates between
({\mathfrak{t}},u,\vartheta)
and
(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2})

makes sense as long as
\mu>0
.

On
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, the standard rotation vector field
\Omega=\epsilon_{i}^{j}x^{i}\partial_{j}
stands
as a tangential derivative.
In order to project
\Omega
on
S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
, as in (3.39b) of
[
28
]
,
one can denote by

R:=\not{\Pi}\Omega,\quad\not{d}:=\not{\Pi}d

the rotation vectorfield and differential of
S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
, respectively. Then

\begin{split}R&=({\not{\Pi}}\Omega)^{i}\partial_{i}=({\not{\Pi}}^{i}_{j}\Omega^{j})\partial_{i}=(\delta_{j}^{k}-g_{ja}\tilde{T}^{a}\tilde{T}^{k}){\Omega}^{j}\partial_{k}=\Omega-g_{ja}\tilde{T}^{a}{\Omega}^{j}\tilde{T}.\end{split}

(2.54)

Set

\upsilon:=g_{ja}\tilde{T}^{a}{\Omega}^{j}=g_{ij}\check{T}^{i}\Omega^{j}.

(2.55)

Then one has

R=\Omega-\upsilon\tilde{T}.

For domains with
\mu>0
, the following integrations and norms
will be utilized repeatedly in subsequent sections.

Definition 2.4
.

For any continuous function
f
, set

\displaystyle\int_{S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}f:=\int_{S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}fd\nu_{\not{g}}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f(\mathfrak{t},u,\vartheta)\sqrt{\not{g}_{XX}(\mathfrak{t},u,\vartheta)}d\vartheta,\qquad\|f\|_{L^{2}(S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u})}^{2}:=\int_{S_{\mathfrak{t},u}}|f|^{2},

\displaystyle\int_{C^{\mathfrak{t}}_{u}}f:=\int_{t_{0}}^{\mathfrak{t}}\int_{S_{\tau,u}}f(\tau,u,\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}d\tau,\qquad\|f\|_{L^{2}(C^{\mathfrak{t}}_{u})}^{2}:=\int_{C^{\mathfrak{t}}_{u}}|f|^{2},

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}^{u}}f:=\int_{0}^{u}\int_{S_{{\mathfrak{t}},u^{\prime}}}f({\mathfrak{t}},u^{\prime},\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}du^{\prime},\qquad\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}^{u})}^{2}:=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}^{u}}|f|^{2},

\displaystyle\int_{D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}f:=\int_{t_{0}}^{\mathfrak{t}}\int_{0}^{u}\int_{S_{\tau,u^{\prime}}}f(\tau,u^{\prime},\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}du^{\prime}d\tau,\quad\|f\|_{L^{2}(D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u})}^{2}:=\int_{D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}}|f|^{2}.

For reader’s convenience, the notation of contractions is recalled as follows:

Definition 2.5
.

If
\Theta
is a
(0,2)
-type spacetime tensor,
\kappa
is a one-form,
U
and
V
are vector fields, the contraction
of
\Theta
with respect to
U
and
V
is then defined as

\Theta_{UV}:=\Theta_{\alpha\beta}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta},

and the contraction of
\kappa
with respect to
U
is

\kappa_{U}:=\kappa_{\alpha}U^{\alpha}.

If
\xi
is a
(0,2)
-type tensor on
S_{\mathfrak{t},u}
, then the trace of
\xi
is defined as

\text{tr}\xi:=\not{g}^{XX}\xi_{XX}.

2.3
Basic equalities in the null frames

In this subsection, some basic equalities in the frame
\{\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}},X\}

or
\{T,\mathring{\underline{L}},X\}
will be given. Set

\begin{split}&\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}:=\frac{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial\varphi_{\gamma}}\quad\text{and}\quad\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma\nu}:=\frac{\partial G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}}{\partial\varphi_{\nu}}.\end{split}

For any vector fields
\displaystyle U=U^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\alpha}}
and
\displaystyle V=V^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\alpha}}
, as in Definition
2.5
, one can define
G_{UV}^{\gamma}:=G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta}
and

G_{UV}^{\gamma\nu}:=G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma\nu}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta}
. Direct computations yield

\begin{split}&G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}=-2c^{-1}\mathring{L}^{\gamma},\quad G_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}^{0}=2c^{-1},\quad G_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}^{a}=2c^{-1}\varphi_{a}-c^{-1}\tilde{T}^{a},\\
&G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{0}=-2c^{-1},\quad G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{a}=-2c^{-1}\varphi_{a},\quad G_{\mathring{L}X}^{0}=0,\quad G_{\mathring{L}X}^{a}=-c^{-1}\not{d}_{X}x^{a},\\
&G_{\tilde{T}X}^{\gamma}=0,\quad G_{XX}^{0}=-2c^{-1}\not{g}_{XX},\quad G_{XX}^{a}=-2c^{-1}\varphi_{a}\not{g}_{XX}\end{split}

(2.56)

and

\begin{split}&G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{00}=8c^{-2},\quad G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{0a}=4c^{-2}(\varphi_{a}+\mathring{L}^{a}),\quad G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{ab}=4c^{-2}(\varphi_{a}\mathring{L}^{b}+\varphi_{b}\mathring{L}^{a}),\\
&G_{XX}^{00}=8c^{-2}\not{g}_{XX},\quad G_{XX}^{0a}=8c^{-2}\varphi_{a}\not{g}_{XX},\quad G_{XX}^{ab}=2c^{-2}(4\varphi_{a}\varphi_{b}-c\delta_{ab})\not{g}_{XX},\\
&G_{\mathring{L}X}^{00}=0,\quad G_{\mathring{L}X}^{0a}=2c^{-2}\not{d}_{X}x^{a},\quad G_{\mathring{L}X}^{ab}=2c^{-2}(\varphi_{a}\not{d}_{X}x^{b}+\varphi_{a}\not{d}_{X}x^{a}),\\
&G_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}^{00}=-8c^{-2},\quad G_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}^{0a}=2c^{-2}(\tilde{T}^{a}-4\varphi_{a}),\quad G_{\mathring{L}\tilde{T}}^{ab}=2c^{-2}(c\delta_{ab}-4\varphi_{a}\varphi_{b}+\varphi_{a}\tilde{T}^{b}+\varphi_{b}\tilde{T}^{a}),\\
&G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{00}=8c^{-2},\quad G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{0a}=8c^{-2}\varphi_{a},\quad G_{\tilde{T}\tilde{T}}^{ab}=2c^{-2}(4\varphi_{a}\varphi_{b}-c\delta_{ab}),\quad G_{\tilde{T}X}^{\alpha\beta}=0,\end{split}

(2.57)

where
\not{d}_{X}f=X^{i}{\not{d}}_{i}f
and
{\not{d}}_{i}f={\not{\Pi}}_{i}^{\alpha}(d_{\alpha}f)={\not{\Pi}}_{i}^{\alpha}(\partial_{\alpha}f)

for any smooth function
f
.

It follows from (
2.56
) that the following lemma holds.

Lemma 2.3
.

\mu
satisfies

\begin{split}\mathring{L}\mu=c^{-1}\mu\big(\mathring{L}^{\alpha}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\alpha}-\mathring{L}c\big).\end{split}

(2.58)

Proof.

The proof is exactly same as those in
[
11
, Lemma 4.3]
, one can also take the explicit expression (
2.56
)
into
[
11
, (4.18)]
to get (
2.58
).
∎

Remark 2.2
.

As in
[
11
]
, the importance of the expression (
2.58
) should be emphasized here. Due to both the null conditions and the special
structures of equation (
1.8
),

\mathring{L}\mu
is just a combination of
\mathring{L}\varphi_{\alpha}
(note that
\mathring{L}c=2\mathring{L}\varphi_{0}+2\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\varphi_{i}\mathring{L}\varphi_{i}
). Based on this and the smallness and some suitable time decay rate
of
\mathring{L}\varphi_{\alpha}
, we will be able to show that
\mu\geq C
for some positive constant
C
(see (
3.80
)
in Section
3
). This is in contrast to the cases in
[
4
,
22
,
25
,
28
]
, where
\mathring{L}\mu

contains the factor
T\varphi_{\alpha}
which leads to
\mu\rightarrow 0+
in finite time (e.g.
[
25
, (2.36)]
).

Note that the deformation tensor defined in (
2.51
)
will occur in the subsequent energy estimates. It is necessary to check the components
of
{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(V)}\pi
in the null frame
\{\mathring{L},\underline{\mathring{L}},X\}
.

Let
{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(V)}\not{\pi}_{UX}={\vphantom{\pi}}^{(V)}\pi_{UX}
for
U\in\{\mathring{L},\underline{\mathring{L}},X\}
.
Following the computations in
[
28
, Proposition 7.7]
or
[
11
, (4.21)-(4.23)]
, one has

(1)

for
V=T
,

\begin{split}&{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(T)}\pi_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}=0,\quad{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(T)}\pi_{T\tilde{T}}=2T\mu,\quad{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(T)}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}=-T\mu,\quad{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(T)}\not{\pi}_{TX}=0,\\
&{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(T)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}=-\not{d}_{X}\mu-2c^{-1}\mu\tilde{T}^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a},\quad{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(T)}\not{\pi}_{XX}=2\mu\tilde{\theta}_{XX};\end{split}

(2.59)

(2)

for
V=\mathring{L}
,

\begin{split}&{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(\mathring{L})}\pi_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}=0,\quad{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(\mathring{L})}\pi_{T\tilde{T}}=2\mathring{L}\mu,\quad{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(\mathring{L})}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}=-\mathring{L}\mu,\quad{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(\mathring{L})}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}=0,\\
&{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(\mathring{L})}\not{\pi}_{TX}=\not{d}_{X}\mu+2c^{-1}\mu\tilde{T}^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a},\quad{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(\mathring{L})}\not{\pi}_{XX}=2\chi_{XX};\end{split}

(2.60)

(3)

for
V=R
,

\begin{split}&{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(R)}\pi_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}=0,\quad{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(R)}\pi_{T\tilde{T}}=2R\mu,\quad{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(R)}\pi_{\mathring{L}T}=-R\mu,\\
&{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}=-{R}^{X}\check{\chi}_{XX}+g_{aj}\epsilon_{i}^{j}\check{L}^{i}\not{d}_{X}x^{a}+\upsilon c^{-1}\tilde{T}^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}R^{X}\not{g}_{XX}(\mathring{L}c)-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\upsilon\not{d}_{X}c\\
&{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{TX}=\mu{R}^{X}\check{\chi}_{XX}+\upsilon\not{d}_{X}\mu+\mu g_{aj}\epsilon_{i}^{j}\check{T}^{i}\not{d}_{X}x^{a}+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\mu R^{X}\not{g}_{XX}(\mathring{L}c)-c^{-1}\mu(R\varphi_{a})\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\\
&\qquad\quad\quad+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\mu\upsilon\not{d}_{X}c,\\
&{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{XX}=2\upsilon\chi_{XX}+c^{-1}\upsilon(\mathring{L}c)\not{g}_{XX}-2c^{-1}\upsilon(\not{d}_{X}x^{a})\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}-c^{-1}(Rc)\not{g}_{XX}.\end{split}

(2.61)

As seen in (
2.58
) and (
2.59
)-(
2.61
),
the components of
\mathring{L}
and
\tilde{T}
appear frequently. In view of
\tilde{T}^{i}=\varphi_{i}-\mathring{L}^{i}
,
one can find the equations for
\mathring{L}^{i}
and
\check{L}^{i}
under the null frame
\{T,\mathring{L},X\}
as follows.

Lemma 2.4
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\mathring{L}\mathring{L}^{i}=-c^{-1}\mathring{L}^{\alpha}(\mathring{L}\varphi_{\alpha})\tilde{T}^{i},

(2.62)

\displaystyle\mathring{L}\big(\varrho\check{L}^{i}\big)=\varrho\mathring{L}\mathring{L}^{i}=-c^{-1}\varrho\mathring{L}^{\alpha}(\mathring{L}\varphi_{\alpha})\tilde{T}^{i},

(2.63)

\displaystyle\not{d}_{X}\mathring{L}^{i}=(\textrm{tr}\chi){\not{d}}_{X}x^{i}+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\mathring{L}c)\not{d}_{X}x^{i}-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\not{d}_{X}c)\tilde{T}^{i}+c^{-1}\tilde{T}^{a}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\tilde{T}^{i},

(2.64)

\displaystyle\not{d}_{X}\check{L}^{i}=(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}){\not{d}}_{X}x^{i}+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\mathring{L}c)\not{d}_{X}x^{i}-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\not{d}_{X}c)\tilde{T}^{i}+c^{-1}\tilde{T}^{a}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\tilde{T}^{i},

(2.65)

where
\not{d}^{X}f={\not{g}}^{XX}{\not{d}}_{X}f={\not{g}}^{XX}(Xf)
for any smooth function
f
.

Proof.

This follows directly from (
2.56
) as for the proof of
[
28
, Proposition 4.7]
.
∎

In addition, it follows from (
2.49
)-(
2.50
), (
2.56
) and Lemma
2.4

that

\displaystyle\zeta_{X}=c^{-1}\tilde{T}^{a}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}),\qquad\xi_{X}=c^{-1}\mu\tilde{T}^{a}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})+\not{d}_{X}\mu,

(2.66)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{XX}=-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\mu^{-1}(Tc)\not{g}_{XX}-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\mathring{L}c)\not{g}_{XX}+c^{-1}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\not{d}_{X}x^{a}-\chi_{XX},

(2.67)

which have been given in
[
28
, Lemma 5.1]
in terms of
G_{XY}
.
Then (
2.66
) implies that

\begin{split}&T\mathring{L}^{i}=-c^{-1}(\tilde{T}^{a}\mathring{L}\varphi_{a})T^{i}+(\not{d}_{X}\mu)(\not{d}^{X}x^{i})+c^{-1}\mu(\tilde{T}^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\not{d}^{X}x^{i}+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\mu(\not{d}^{X}c)(\not{d}_{X}x^{i}).\end{split}

(2.68)

For later analysis, one also needs the following connection coefficients in the new frames, which are given
in Lemmas 5.1 and 5.3 of
[
28
]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

The covariant derivatives in the frame
\{T,\mathring{L},X\}
are

\begin{split}&\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{L}}\mathring{L}=(\mu^{-1}\mathring{L}\mu)\mathring{L},\quad\mathscr{D}_{T}\mathring{L}=-\mathring{L}\mu\mathring{L}+\xi^{X}X,\quad\mathscr{D}_{X}\mathring{L}=-\zeta_{X}\mathring{L}+{\textrm{tr}\chi}X,\\
&\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{L}}T=-\mathring{L}\mu\mathring{L}-\mu\zeta^{X}X,\quad\mathscr{D}_{T}T=\mu\mathring{L}\mu\mathring{L}+(\mu^{-1}T\mu+\mathring{L}\mu)T-\mu(\not{d}^{X}\mu)X,\\
&\mathscr{D}_{X}T=\mu\zeta_{X}\mathring{L}+\mu^{-1}\xi_{X}T+(\mu\textrm{tr}\tilde{\theta})X,\\
&\mathscr{D}_{X}X=\not{\nabla}_{X}X+(\tilde{\theta}_{XX}+\chi_{XX})\mathring{L}+\mu^{-1}\chi_{XX}T.\end{split}

(2.69)

Lemma 2.6
.

The covariant derivatives in the frame
\{\mathring{\underline{L}},\mathring{L},X\}
are

\begin{split}&\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}}{\mathring{L}}=-\mathring{L}\mu\mathring{L}+2\xi^{X}X,\quad\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{L}}\mathring{\underline{L}}=-2\mu\zeta^{X}X,\\
&\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{\underline{L}}}\mathring{\underline{L}}=(\mu^{-1}\mathring{\underline{L}}\mu+\mathring{L}\mu)\mathring{\underline{L}}-(2\mu\not{d}^{X}\mu)X.\end{split}

(2.70)

Next we derive the equation for
\varphi_{\gamma}
under the action of the covariant wave operator
\Box_{g}
.
With the metric
g
given in (
1.19
), one has

\begin{split}\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=&\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\text{det}g|}}\partial_{\alpha}\big(\sqrt{|\text{det}g|}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\varphi_{\gamma}\big)\\
=&-c^{-1}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}c(\partial_{\beta}\varphi_{\gamma})+g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}\\
&-\sum_{i}\partial_{i}\varphi_{i}(\partial_{t}+\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}\partial_{j})\varphi_{\gamma}+\sum_{i}\partial_{i}\varphi_{\gamma}(\partial_{t}+\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}\partial_{j})\varphi_{i}.\end{split}

(2.71)

Differentiating (
1.8
) with respect to the variable
x^{\gamma}
yields

\begin{split}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}=2\sum_{i}\partial_{i}\varphi_{\gamma}(\partial_{t}+\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}\partial_{j})\varphi_{i}-2\sum_{i}\partial_{i}\varphi_{i}(\partial_{t}+\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}\partial_{j})\varphi_{\gamma}.\end{split}

(2.72)

Substituting (
2.72
) into (
2.71
) leads to

\begin{split}\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=&-c^{-1}g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial_{\alpha}c)(\partial_{\beta}\varphi_{\gamma})\\
&-3\sum_{i}\partial_{i}\varphi_{i}(\partial_{t}+\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}\partial_{j})\varphi_{\gamma}+3\sum_{i}\partial_{i}\varphi_{\gamma}(\partial_{t}+\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}\partial_{j})\varphi_{i}.\end{split}

(2.73)

Note that

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}=-\mathring{L}^{\alpha}\mathring{L}^{\beta}-\tilde{T}^{\alpha}\mathring{L}^{\beta}-\mathring{L}^{\alpha}\tilde{T}^{\beta}+(\not{d}_{X}x^{\alpha})(\not{d}^{X}x^{\beta}),

(2.74)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}=c^{-1}\mu^{-1}\tilde{T}^{i}T+c^{-1}(\not{d}^{X}x^{i})X,

(2.75)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}+\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}\partial_{j}=\mathring{L}+\mu^{-1}T.

(2.76)

Then in the frame
\{T,\mathring{L},X\}
, (
2.73
) can be rewritten as

\begin{split}\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=&c^{-1}\big\{\frac{1}{2}\mu\mathring{L}c+Tc-3\tilde{T}^{i}T\varphi_{i}-\frac{3}{2}\mu\tilde{T}^{i}\mathring{L}\varphi_{i}-\frac{3}{2}\mu\not{d}^{X}x^{i}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{i})\big\}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\big\{\mathring{L}c+3\tilde{T}^{i}\mathring{L}\varphi_{i}-3\not{d}^{X}x^{i}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{i})\big\}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}\\
&+c^{-1}\big\{-\mu\not{d}^{X}c+3\mu(\mathring{L}\varphi_{i})\not{d}^{X}x^{i}+3(T\varphi_{i})\not{d}^{X}x^{i}\big\}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma},\end{split}

(2.77)

where
\gamma=0,1,2.

For later use, some identities involving commutators are given in the following lemmas, which are proved in
Lemmas 4.10, 8.9 and 8.11 of
[
28
]
for the 3D case.

Lemma 2.7
.

In the frame
\{\mathring{L},T,R\}
, it holds that

\begin{split}[\mathring{L},R]&={{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}}}^{X}X,\ \ [\mathring{L},T]={{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(T)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}}}^{X}X,\ \ [T,R]={{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{T}}^{X}X,\end{split}

(2.78)

where
{{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}}}^{X}={\not{g}}^{XX}{{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}}

and
{{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(R)}\not{\pi}_{\mathring{L}X}}={{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(R)}\pi_{\mathring{L}X}}={{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(R)}\pi_{\alpha\beta}{\mathring{L}}^{\alpha}X^{\beta}}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

For any vector field
Z\in\{\mathring{L},T,R\}
,

1.

if
f
is a smooth function, then

\big([\not{\nabla}^{2},\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}]f\big)_{XX}=\frac{1}{2}{\not{\nabla}}_{X}\big(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}\big)\not{d}_{X}f;

2.

if
\Theta
is a one-form on
S_{\mathfrak{t},u}
, then

([\not{\nabla}_{X},\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}]\Theta)_{X}=\frac{1}{2}{\not{\nabla}}_{X}\big(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}\big)\Theta_{X};

3.

if
\Theta
is a
(0,2)
-type tensor on
S_{\mathfrak{t},u}
, then

\displaystyle([\not{\nabla}_{X},\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}]\Theta)_{XX}

\displaystyle={\not{\nabla}}_{X}\big(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}\big)\Theta_{XX},

\displaystyle([\not{\nabla}_{X},\not{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}]\not{\nabla}\Theta)_{XXX}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}{\not{\nabla}}_{X}\big(\textrm{tr}{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}\big)\not{\nabla}_{X}\Theta_{XX},

where
{{\vphantom{\not{\pi}}}{}^{(Z)}\not{\pi}_{XX}}={{\vphantom{\pi}}{}^{(Z)}\pi_{XX}}
.

3
Bootstrap assumptions on
\partial\phi
near
C_{0}
and some related estimates

We will derive the global estimates of a solution to (
1.8
) near
C_{0}
by a bootstrap argument. To this end, for any given smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.8
), we assume that in
D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}\subset A_{2\delta}
:

\begin{split}&\delta^{l}\|(\mathfrak{t}\mathring{L}Z^{\alpha}\varphi_{\gamma},\mathfrak{t}\not{d}Z^{\alpha}\varphi_{\gamma},\delta\mathring{\underline{L}}Z^{\alpha}\varphi_{\gamma})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}^{u})}\leq M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-1/2},\\
&\|(\mathfrak{t}^{2}\not{\nabla}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma},\varphi_{\gamma})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}^{u})}\leq M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-1/2},\end{split}

(
\star
)

where
|\alpha|\leq N
,
N
is a fixed large positive integer,
M
is a positive constant to be chosen later
(at least double bounds of the corresponding quantities on time
t_{0}
),

Z\in\{\varrho\mathring{L},T,R\}
, and
l
is the number of
T
included in
Z^{\alpha}
.

We start with a rough estimate of
\mu
under assumptions (
\star
). Note that

1=g_{ij}\tilde{T}^{i}\tilde{T}^{j}=\big(1+O(M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-1/2})\big)\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\tilde{T}^{i}|^{2}

by (
2.46
). This means

|\tilde{T}^{i}|,|\mathring{L}^{i}|\leq 1+O(M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-1/2})

(3.79)

due to
\mathring{L}^{i}=\varphi_{i}-\tilde{T}^{i}
. This, together with
(\star)
and (
2.58
), implies
|\mathring{L}\mu|\lesssim M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-3/2}\mu
. When
\delta>0
is small, by
integrating
\mathring{L}\mu
along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
and noting
\mu=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}=1+O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}})
on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
,
one can get directly that

\mu=1+O(M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}).

(3.80)

To improve the estimates and close the assumptions (
\star
), one may rewrite (
2.77
) in the frame

\{\mathring{\underline{L}},\mathring{L},X\}
as

\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\varrho}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}=H_{\gamma},

(3.81)

due to the fact that
\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=-\mathscr{D}_{\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}+\mu\not{g}^{XX}\mathscr{D}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma}=\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}-2\mu\zeta^{X}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}+\mu\not{\triangle}\varphi_{\gamma}-\mu(\textrm{tr}\tilde{\theta}+\textrm{tr}\chi)\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}-\textrm{tr}\chi T\varphi_{\gamma}

by (
2.69
) and (
2.70
). In addition, by (
2.66
)-(
2.67
),

\begin{split}H_{\gamma}=&\mu\not{\triangle}\varphi_{\gamma}+\big\{-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}Tc+3c^{-1}\tilde{T}^{a}T\varphi_{a}+\frac{3}{2}c^{-1}\mu\tilde{T}^{a}\mathring{L}\varphi_{a}+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\mu(\not{d}^{X}x^{a})\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\mu\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}+\frac{\mu}{2\varrho}\big\}\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}+\big\{-\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\mathring{L}c-\frac{3}{2}c^{-1}\tilde{T}^{a}\mathring{L}\varphi_{a}+\frac{3}{2}c^{-1}(\not{d}^{X}x^{a})\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}\big\}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}\\
&-c^{-1}\mu\big\{3\tilde{T}^{a}+\mathring{L}^{\alpha}\big\}(\not{d}^{X}\varphi_{\alpha})\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{\gamma}.\end{split}

(3.82)

Note from the expression of
H_{\gamma}
that the terms containing
factors
T\varphi_{\alpha}
or
{\mathring{\underline{L}}}\varphi_{\alpha}
,
which are not small “enough” and have slow decay rate in time, appear always with some accompanying factors
\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}
or
\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}
with the “good”
smallness and fast time decay rate (see
-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(Tc)(\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma})
and

-\frac{1}{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}(\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma})
in
H_{\gamma}
). This implies that
H_{\gamma}
may possess
some desired properties helpful for our analysis later.

Unless stated otherwise, from now on until Section
6
, the pointwise estimates for the corresponding quantities
are all carried out inside the domain
D^{{\mathfrak{t}},u}
.

To estimate
\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}
by integrating (
3.81
)
along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
, one needs to estimate
\not{d}x^{i}
,
\check{\chi}
and so on in

H_{\gamma}
first.

Note that
|\not{d}x^{i}|^{2}=\not{g}^{ab}\not{d}_{a}x^{i}\not{d}_{b}x^{i}=c-(\tilde{T}^{i})^{2}
.
Then
(\star)
and (
3.79
) imply that

|\not{d}x^{i}|\lesssim 1.

(3.83)

To estimate
\check{\chi}
, as in
[
11
]
, one can derive the governing equations for
\check{\chi}
first. Exactly by the
same procedure of proof in
[
11
, Lemma 5.2]
, we have

Lemma 3.1
.

The second fundamental form
\chi
and its “error” form
\check{\chi}
, defined in (
2.49
) and (
2.52
)
respectively, satisfy the following structure equations:

\begin{split}\mathring{L}\chi_{XX}&=c^{-1}\not{d}_{X}x^{a}(\not{d}_{X}\mathring{L}\varphi_{a})-c^{-1}\not{g}_{XX}\big(\mathring{L}^{2}\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{a}\mathring{L}^{2}\varphi_{a}\big)-c^{-1}\mathring{L}^{\gamma}(\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma})+(\textrm{tr}\chi)\chi_{XX}\\
&-c^{-1}\textrm{tr}\chi(\not{d}_{X}x^{a})\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\mathring{L}c)\chi_{XX}+c^{-2}f(\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2},\varphi)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi)(\not{d}_{X}\varphi)\\
\not{g}^{XX}(\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})^{2}\\
\not{g}^{XX}(\mathring{L}\varphi)(\mathring{L}\varphi)\\
(\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\mathring{L}\varphi\end{array}\right),\end{split}

(3.84)

\begin{split}\not{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\chi&{}_{XX}=\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\mu-\mu(\textrm{tr}\chi)\chi_{XX}-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\tilde{T}^{a}T\varphi_{a}+Tc)\chi_{XX}+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\mu(\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\textrm{tr}\chi\\
&+c^{-1}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\not{d}_{X}x^{a}(\not{d}_{X}T\varphi_{a})-\not{g}_{XX}(\mathring{L}T\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{a}\mathring{L}T\varphi_{a})+\mu\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{0}+\mu(\varphi_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{T}^{a})\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{a}\Big\}\\
&+\frac{3}{2}c^{-1}(\tilde{T}^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\not{d}_{X}\mu+c^{-2}f_{1}(\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2},\varphi)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu\mathring{L}\varphi\\
T{\varphi}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\not{g}_{XX}\mathring{L}\vec{\varphi}\\
\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\cdot\not{d}\varphi_{a}\\
\end{array}\right)\\
&+c^{-2}f_{2}(\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2},\varphi)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu(\not{d}_{X}\varphi)(\not{d}_{X}\varphi)\\
\not{g}^{XX}\mu(\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}\varphi_{a})^{2}\\
\end{array}\right),\\
\end{split}

(3.85)

and then

\begin{split}\mathring{L}\check{\chi}_{XX}=&c^{-1}\not{d}_{X}x^{a}(\not{d}_{X}\mathring{L}\varphi_{a})-c^{-1}\not{g}_{XX}\big(\mathring{L}^{2}\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{a}\mathring{L}^{2}\varphi_{a}\big)-c^{-1}\mathring{L}^{\gamma}(\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{\gamma})-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\mathring{L}c)\check{\chi}_{XX}\\
&-c^{-1}(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi})(\not{d}_{X}x^{a})\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}-\frac{1}{2}\varrho^{-1}c^{-1}(\mathring{L}c)\not{g}_{XX}-\varrho^{-1}c^{-1}(\not{d}_{X}x^{a})\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}+(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi})\check{\chi}_{XX}\\
&+c^{-2}f(\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2},\varphi)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}(\not{d}_{X}\varphi)(\not{d}_{X}\varphi)\\
\not{g}^{XX}(\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})^{2}\\
\not{g}^{XX}(\mathring{L}\varphi)(\mathring{L}\varphi)\\
(\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\mathring{L}\varphi\end{array}\right),\end{split}

(3.86)

\begin{split}\not{\mathcal{L}}_{T}&\check{\chi}_{XX}=\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\mu-\mu(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi})\check{\chi}_{XX}-\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}(\tilde{T}^{a}T\varphi_{a}+Tc)\chi_{XX}+\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}\mu(\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\textrm{tr}\chi\\
&+c^{-1}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\not{d}_{X}x^{a}(\not{d}_{X}T\varphi_{a})-\not{g}_{XX}(\mathring{L}T\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{a}\mathring{L}T\varphi_{a})+\mu\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{0}+\mu(\varphi_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{T}^{a})\not{\nabla}_{X}^{2}\varphi_{a}\Big\}\\
&+\frac{3}{2}c^{-1}(\tilde{T}^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a})\not{d}_{X}\mu+\frac{c^{-1}}{\varrho}(Tc)\not{g}_{XX}+\frac{c^{-1}\mu}{\varrho}(\mathring{L}c)\not{g}_{XX}-\frac{2c^{-1}\mu}{\varrho}\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}_{X}\varphi_{a}+\frac{\mu-1}{\varrho^{2}}\not{g}_{XX}\\
&+c^{-2}f_{1}(\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2},\varphi)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu\mathring{L}\varphi\\
T{\varphi}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\not{g}_{XX}\mathring{L}\varphi\\
\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\cdot\not{d}\varphi_{a}\\
\end{array}\right)+c^{-2}f_{2}(\mathring{L}^{1},\mathring{L}^{2},\varphi)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mu(\not{d}_{X}\varphi)(\not{d}_{X}\varphi)\\
\not{g}^{XX}\mu(\not{d}_{X}x^{a}\not{d}\varphi_{a})^{2}\end{array}\right).\end{split}

(3.87)

Based on
\mathring{L}\check{\chi}
in (
3.86
), the estimate of
\check{\chi}
could be achieved
by integrating along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Under the assumptions
(\star)
with
\delta>0
suitably small, it holds that

|\check{\chi}|=|\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}|\lesssim M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-3/2}

(3.88)

and

|\chi|=\frac{1}{\varrho}+O(M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-3/2}).

(3.89)

Proof.

It follows from

\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}=\not{g}^{XX}\check{\chi}_{XX}
that

\mathring{L}\big(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}\big)=-2(\textrm{tr}\check{\chi})^{2}-\frac{2}{\rho}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}+\not{g}^{XX}\mathring{L}\check{\chi}_{XX}.

(3.90)

Substituting (
3.86
) into (
3.90
) and using
(\star)
, (
3.79
) and (
3.83
) to estimate
the right hand side of (
3.90
) except
\check{\chi}
itself, one can get

|\mathring{L}\big(\varrho^{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}\big)|\lesssim M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-5/2}\varrho^{2}+M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-3/2}|\varrho^{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}|+\varrho^{-2}|\varrho^{2}\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}|^{2}.

Thus, for small
\delta>0
, (
3.88
) follows from integrating along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
, which
also yields (
3.89
) directly due to (
2.52
).
∎

It follows from Proposition
3.1
, (
2.64
) and (
2.65
) that for small
\delta>0
,

|\not{d}\mathring{L}^{i}|\lesssim\varrho^{-1},\quad|\not{d}\check{L}^{i}|\lesssim M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-3/2}.

(3.91)

Note that

\mathring{L}\big(\varrho^{2}|\not{d}\mu|^{2}\big)=2\varrho^{2}\big\{-\textrm{tr}\check{\chi}|\not{d}\mu|^{2}+(\not{d}_{X}\mathring{L}\mu)(\not{d}^{X}\mu)\big\}.

(3.92)

Substituting (
2.58
) into (
3.92
) and applying
(\star)
, (
3.1
) and (
3.91
) give

|\mathring{L}\big(\varrho|\not{d}\mu|\big)|\lesssim M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-3/2}\big(\varrho|\not{d}\mu|\big)+M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-3/2}.

This implies immediately that

|\not{d}\mu|\lesssim M\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-1}.

(3.93)

Now we are ready to improve the estimate on
\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}
, which will
lead to better estimates for
\mathring{L}\varphi_{\gamma}

and some other related quantities independent of
M
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Under the assumptions
(\star)
with
\delta>0
suitably small, it holds that

|\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}(\mathfrak{t},u,\vartheta)|+|T\varphi_{\gamma}(\mathfrak{t},u,\vartheta)|\lesssim\delta^{-\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-1/2}.

(3.94)

Proof.

By
(\star)
, (
3.79
), (
3.83
) and (
3.88
), it follows from (
3.81
) that

|\mathring{L}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\varrho}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}|\lesssim M^{2}\delta^{1-2\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-2}.

This yields

|\mathring{L}\big(\varrho^{1/2}\mathring{\underline{L}}\varphi_{\gamma}\big)(\mathfrak{t},u,\vartheta)|\lesssim M^{2}\delta^{1-2\varepsilon_{0}}\mathfrak{t}^{-3/2}.

(3.95)

Integrating (
3.95
) along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
gives
